Never. \n\nMy hearing is perfectly fine, actually. I mean, I get my hearing checked every year at the employee health fair, and it's always 100% normal. And my vision is 20/20. Well, okay, I wear reading glasses sometimes when I'm staring at the spreadsheets for too long, just because the blue light from the monitor gives me a bit of eye strain if I'm up until 2:00 or 3:00 in the morning tracking the timestamp discrepancies. But I don't see things that aren't there. I don't hear things that aren't there. \n\nEverything I'm talking about—the lab results, the LIS system, the portal updates—it's all real, physical data. It's tangible. I have printed copies of the electronic health record. I have the reference ranges highlighted in yellow, and the altered values circled in red. I can show you the exact IP address logs if I could just get IT to release them to me, which they won't, of course, because that would prove I'm right. But none of this is in my head. I'm not sitting in a dark room listening to imaginary voices tell me there's a conspiracy. I'm sitting at my kitchen table, under a very bright, very real fluorescent light, looking at actual, documented proof that someone went into my dad's chart and changed his numbers. So, no. No voices, no visions. Just a lot of very real, very frustrating data that no one else wants to look at.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever told you that your thoughts were being broadcast, inserted, or controlled by someone else?\n\nPatient: No. No, definitely not. Broadcast? Like... like someone could hear what I'm thinking? Or that someone is putting thoughts into my brain? No. No, my thoughts are entirely my own. I mean, honestly, if someone were controlling my thoughts, do you think they'd have me spending every waking moment outside of work agonizing over my dad's metabolic panel? I don't think so. I think if someone were controlling me, they'd probably have me thinking about something a lot more relaxing, like... I don't know, a vacation to Hawaii or something. \n\nBut no, everything I think, everything I conclude—it all comes from me. It comes from my own brain, my own observations, my own background in the medical field. I know how to process information. I took anatomy, I took physiology, I took medical terminology when I was getting my phlebotomy certification. I know how to think critically. So when I sit down with those spreadsheets—and I have about four different Excel workbooks going right now, cross-referencing the dates, the times, the specific tests that were ordered versus what was resulted—that's all my own analytical process. \n\nNobody is putting those thoughts in my head. And I'm certainly not broadcasting them to anyone, either. Although, frankly, I wish I could broadcast them sometimes, just so my family would finally understand what I'm seeing! If I could just beam my thoughts directly into my sister's head, maybe she would stop rolling her eyes at me and actually look at the discrepancies in the creatinine levels. But no, obviously that's impossible. Human brains don't work like radio towers. We don't transmit signals. We're just... we're enclosed systems. So no, my thoughts are my own, my mind is my own, and the only reason I'm so focused on this is because of the evidence in front of me, not because anyone is controlling me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your dad’s lab results, do you generally trust other people, or do you often feel that people are trying to deceive or harm you?\n\nPatient: Trust other people? Oh, I mean, yes. Yes, generally speaking, I trust people just fine. I'm not... I don't walk around thinking the whole world is out to get me. I'm not paranoid, if that's what you're asking. I know there's a difference between someone making a deliberate, calculated alteration to a specific electronic health record and, like, the barista at Starbucks trying to poison my latte. I don't think my neighbors are spying on me, I don't think the government is tapping my phone... well, I mean, maybe they tap some people's phones, but not mine. I'm just a phlebotomist. I go to work, I draw blood, I come home. There's no reason for anyone to be after me.\n\nI trust my coworkers, for the most part. I mean, Sarah at the front desk can be a little gossipy, and sometimes I think she takes longer lunches than she clocks out for, but I don't think she's trying to harm me. And I trust my patients. I have to, right? I have to trust that they're telling me the truth when they say they fasted for their lipid panel, even if the serum looks a little lipemic when I spin it down. You have to have a baseline level of trust to function in society, and especially in healthcare. \n\nIt's just... it's just this one specific thing. This one instance with my dad's chart. Because I saw the discrepancy. It's not about me distrusting the entire medical establishment, it's about me knowing that in this one particular case, with this one particular set of metabolic values, someone intervened. And maybe they didn't even do it to harm him, you know? Maybe it was a mistake they tried to cover up, like I said, or maybe they thought they were correcting an instrument error but they did it wrong. I don't know their motives. I just know the data was changed. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel like people are generally trying to deceive me. I actually think most people are just trying to get through their day, do their jobs, and go home to their families. Which is why it's so frustrating that no one will just look at my spreadsheets! Because if they were just honest, if they just looked at the timeline I've mapped out, they would see it. But outside of that? No. I go to the grocery store, I pay my taxes, I chat with the mailman. I'm perfectly fine with people. It's just the LIS backend that I don't trust.\n\nDoctor: If I showed you clear, verified records proving your dad’s lab values were never changed, would you be able to accept that you might be wrong?\n\nPatient: Verified records? But... but what does \"verified\" even mean in this context? If someone has administrative access to the LIS, the Laboratory Information System, they can alter the audit trail. So if you show me a record, it's just a printout of the altered database. It doesn't prove I'm wrong, it just proves they covered their tracks! \n\nI mean, that is exactly why I'm here, sitting in this chair right now. Because for the past year—an entire year, twelve months of me losing sleep over this—I have been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And it is like talking to a brick wall. No matter how many doctors or family members sit me down and tell me the numbers are fine, or try to show me these so-called verified records, it doesn't change the truth. My sister does it, his primary care doctor does it, and now you're doing it. \n\nBut I know what I saw. I know the chart doesn't match what I saw with my own two eyes that first afternoon when the preliminary results crossed the portal before they were scrubbed. I saw the original values. I know I did. And honestly... it’s wearing me down. It is completely wearing me down. I spend my whole shift at the clinic drawing blood, spinning down tubes, making sure every single label is perfectly aligned, and then I come home and I spend hours trying to get people to just listen to me about my own father's bloodwork. \n\nSo no, a piece of paper that someone printed from a compromised system isn't going to make me accept that I'm wrong, because I'm not wrong. I just need someone to actually look at the raw data from the analyzer instead of telling me to stop looking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had similar suspicions about other people or situations outside of your dad’s lab results?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I was trying to explain a minute ago, this isn't some... some overarching paranoia. I don't have suspicions about other people or other situations because there's no evidence of anything else being wrong! Outside of the dispute about my dad’s records—which, again, is based on hard numbers, not just me making things up—I am completely, entirely functional. \n\nI still keep all my phlebotomy shifts. Actually, I haven't called out sick once in the last fourteen months. Not once. Even when I had that terrible sinus infection back in October, I still went in, wore my mask, and did my job, because my patients need me and my supervisor relies on me. I have the best first-stick success rate in the whole department, by the way. I manage all my normal day-to-day responsibilities without any issue whatsoever. I pay my bills on time—early, usually. I have everything set up on autopay through my credit union so I never miss a due date for my rent or my car insurance. \n\nI take care of myself, too. I do my laundry every Sunday morning without fail, I go grocery shopping, I meal prep my lunches for the week so I'm not buying overpriced cafeteria food... usually just chicken and quinoa or something simple that I can eat in the breakroom. I mean, people look at me, like my sister, and they think, oh, she's got these spreadsheets, she's spending all her free time looking at lab values, her whole life must be falling apart. But it's not! \n\nI am doing everything a responsible adult is supposed to do. I take my car in for its oil changes, I remember my family's birthdays, I water my houseplants. I am managing my life perfectly well. It’s just this one specific situation with the LIS and my dad’s metabolic panel that requires my extra attention, because nobody else is doing anything about it. So, no, I don't suspect the mailman or the grocery clerk or my landlord of anything. My life outside of this one medical discrepancy is completely and utterly normal.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also been feeling depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting around crying or unable to get out of bed, if that's what you mean by feeling down. Like I was just saying, I get up every single day at 5:30 AM, I make my coffee—just black, usually, because I don't want the extra sugar crash later—and I'm out the door by 6:15 so I can get to the clinic and prep my phlebotomy cart before the first fasting patients arrive at 7:00. You just can't do that if you're depressed, right? People who are down all day don't have the energy to maintain a perfect attendance record or make sure their tourniquets and alcohol pads are perfectly stocked and organized by draw order.\n\nI mean, sure, I'm tired. I am incredibly tired. But it's not a sad tired, it's a... a frustrated tired. It's the sheer exhaustion of knowing a piece of data was altered and having everyone look at you like you're speaking a foreign language. When I'm up at 2 AM cross-referencing the metabolic reference ranges from the old system update with the new one, I'm not doing it because I'm depressed. I'm doing it because I have to figure out why those numbers were scrubbed. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel down or hopeless about life in general. I like my job, I like my routine. If I seem stressed or overwhelmed, it’s purely the stress of trying to get to the bottom of my dad's chart, not depression. I just honestly don't have the time to be depressed when there are still so many spreadsheet rows I need to verify.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone has ever put a device, camera, or tracking system on you personally?\n\nPatient: A tracking system? On me? No. No, why would anyone do that? That sounds like something out of a spy movie, honestly. I don't think anyone is following me around or putting cameras in my house or... or bugging my phone or anything like that. \n\nLike I keep trying to tell you, my life is incredibly boring outside of the clinic and this situation with my dad's chart. I go to work, I draw blood, I come home, I eat my chicken and quinoa, I look at my spreadsheets, and I go to sleep. I mean, who would want to watch that? What would they even get out of it? Watching me organize my phlebotomy cart? Watching me water my pothos plant on the windowsill? It just doesn't make any sense. \n\nThe issue isn't that someone is watching me or tracking me. The issue is entirely digital. It's confined completely to the Laboratory Information System and whoever has the administrative credentials to alter the audit trails for the preliminary results. They don't need to track me physically because the cover-up is happening on the servers, not in my apartment. So, no, I don't have any worries about cameras or tracking devices. That's just... that's not what's happening here at all. I really need you to understand that I'm focused on the data, the actual hard data from the analyzer, not some wild idea about being followed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Unusually energized? I mean... no, not really. I wouldn't say I feel unusually energized. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, I'm actually really tired most of the time. I'm exhausted. But it's not! \n\nI am doing everything a responsible adult is supposed to do. I take my car in for its oil changes, I remember my family's birthdays, I water my houseplants. I am managing my life perfectly well. It’s just this one specific situation with the LIS and my dad’s metabolic panel that requires my extra attention, because nobody else is doing anything about it. So, no, I don't suspect the mailman or the grocery clerk or my landlord of anything. My life outside of this one medical discrepancy is completely and utterly normal.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also been feeling depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm not sitting around crying or unable to get out of bed, if that's what you mean by feeling down. Like I was just saying, I get up every single day at 5:30 AM, I make my coffee—just black, usually, because I don't want the extra sugar crash later—and I'm out the door by 6:15 so I can get to the clinic and prep my phlebotomy cart before the first fasting patients arrive at 7:00. You just can't do that if you're depressed, right? People who are down all day don't have the energy to maintain a perfect attendance record or make sure their tourniquets and alcohol pads are perfectly stocked and organized by draw order.\n\nI mean, sure, I'm tired. I am incredibly tired. But it's not a sad tired, it's a... a frustrated tired. It's the sheer exhaustion of knowing a piece of data was altered and having everyone look at you like you're speaking a foreign language. When I'm up at 2 AM cross-referencing the metabolic reference ranges from the old system update with the new one, I'm not doing it because I'm depressed. I'm doing it because I have to figure out why those numbers were scrubbed. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel down or hopeless about life in general. I like my job, I like my routine. If I seem stressed or overwhelmed, it’s purely the stress of trying to get to the bottom of my dad's chart, not depression. I just honestly don't have the time to be depressed when there are still so many spreadsheet rows I need to verify.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone has ever put a device, camera, or tracking system on you personally?\n\nPatient: A tracking system? On me? No. No, why would anyone do that? That sounds like something out of a spy movie, honestly. I don't think anyone is following me around or putting cameras in my house or... or bugging my phone or anything like that. \n\nLike I keep trying to tell you, my life is incredibly boring outside of the clinic and this situation with my dad's chart. That's just... that's not what's happening here at all. I mean... no, not really. I wouldn't say I feel unusually energized. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, I'm actually really tired most of the time. I'm exhausted. It's just that there aren't enough hours in the day to get through everything. \n\nI still set my alarm for 5:30 AM every single morning, like I always do, but lately, I've been staying up until 1:00 or 2:00 in the morning. Sometimes 3:00, if I hit a snag. But it's not because I'm bouncing off the walls or feeling wired. It's because I'm sitting at my kitchen table with my laptop, trying to run macros on these Excel sheets to compare the timestamp discrepancies between the preliminary metabolic panel results and the finalized ones. You wouldn't believe how tedious it is to manually check the delta checks when the LIS audit trail has been wiped. I'd love to just go to bed at 10:00 PM like a normal person. But I can't. I can't just turn my brain off and go to sleep when I know those lab values were changed. If I don't stay up and document every single inconsistency, who will? My sister certainly isn't going to do it. The hospital administration isn't going to do it. \n\nSo, no, it's not an energy thing. If I had my way, I'd sleep for a week straight, but I just don't have that luxury right now. Not until I can prove exactly what happened to my dad's chart.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If I have a 21-gauge needle and a light blue top tube for a coagulation test, that is a real, tangible thing in my hand. There's no ambiguity about it. I don't see things that aren't there, I don't hear voices, I don't... I don't have weird beliefs about ghosts or whatever else people come up with. \n\nSo, no, there is nothing else. And honestly, even with my dad's lab results, it's not an \"experience\" that just feels real to me. It is real. The fact that other people say it's not real—like my sister, who won't even look at the printouts I brought over to her house—that's just because they don't understand how the Laboratory Information System works. They just look at the finalized PDF report the hospital generates and they accept it blindly. They don't know how to check the delta flags or the timestamp revisions in the backend, so to them, it doesn't exist. \n\nI mean, if you took a person who didn't know anything about cars, and you told them the transmission was slipping, they might say, \"Well, the car drives fine to me, so that's not real.\" But a mechanic would know, right? Because they actually look under the hood. That's all I'm doing. I'm looking under the hood of my dad's metabolic panel. \n\nBut outside of that? No, never. My life is completely normal. I pay my rent, I go to the grocery store, I do my laundry on Sundays, I prep my phlebotomy cart... there's absolutely nothing else in my life that people say isn't real. Just this one, highly documented data discrepancy that nobody else wants to take the time to actually look at."
